A curated list of papers that propose new optimizers or improve existing ones, newest first. It covers deep-learning optimizers such as Adam and Muon variants, along with second-order, zeroth-order, memory-efficient and federated methods. Muon gets extra coverage, with companion lists of related studies and of blogs, implementations and other resources.

## Optimizer Papers

### 2026

#### October 2026

| Date | Paper Title | Summary |
| :---: | :---: | :--- |
| 2026-10-16 | [BMuon: Budgeting Muon's Spectral Geometry for Structured Compressibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23571) | Makes MLP hidden channels compete for a global allocation budget, applying coupled channel weights inside and outside Muon's polar maps. A minorize-maximize allocation update uses channel scores and scalar bisection; refreshing every five steps costs 1.2 polar evaluations per budgeted matrix on average. Three-seed vision experiments improve gradual-pruning accuracy. A 1B LLaMA-style model trained on 13.1B FineWeb tokens retains perplexity 12.85 after 80% channel pruning, but dense perplexity is worse than Muon's 10.92. Representation-cost guarantees concern linear factorizations, not training implicit bias. The official repository is currently a README-only release placeholder, so no implementation link is listed. |
| 2026-10-15 | [A Second-Order Descent Algorithm for Nonlinear Equality Constrained Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21673) | Combines sequential quadratic programming with homogeneous second-order descent for nonlinear equality constraints. Each trial splits into a normal feasibility step and a reduced-Hessian tangential step obtained from a generalized homogeneous eigenvalue model; merit-ratio acceptance adapts regularization and handles negative-curvature hard cases. Its epsilon-to-the-minus-three-halves iteration claim requires the explicit structural condition kappa_h=O(1), alongside smoothness and constraint assumptions. Deterministic MATLAB experiments report 81 solved CUTEst problems and favorable evaluation counts against SQP-type baselines. Competitor runtimes are not compared, and Hessians, null-space construction, and eigenvalue solves remain substantial costs; these results do not establish scalable stochastic neural-network training performance. |
| 2026-10-15 | [When is a better LLM worth paying for? Stochastic Optimization with Switching Mixed-Fidelity Oracles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21139) | Introduces cost-aware stochastic-optimization wrappers that switch among biased, mixed-fidelity gradient oracles. With known bias, restarted stages choose the cheapest admissible contraction, using biased stochastic gradient extrapolation as one realization; with unknown bias, sparse trusted-gradient certification rejects unsuccessful sources. Guarantees assume smooth strongly convex objectives, state-dependent noise, and valid oracle admissibility bounds. Synthetic regression and LLM-based missing-covariate completion illustrate lower equivalent information cost than fixed-fidelity choices. Experiments use clean-Hessian whitening and a practical bias proxy rather than the sufficient worst-case rule; completion prices are amortized accounting costs, not measured end-to-end neural-model training speedups. No substantive official implementation was located. |
| 2026-10-14 | [Envelope Cubic Regularized Newton Method for Nonconvex-Strongly-Concave Minimax Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20345) | ECRN applies inexact cubic regularized Newton to a regularized primal envelope and uses a NewtonTrack inner solver to approximate its derivatives. A sharper envelope Hessian-Lipschitz estimate improves condition-number dependence to kappa-to-the-three-quarters in second-order oracle complexity, matching a deterministic lower bound up to logarithmic factors. Robust logistic-regression experiments on three LIBSVM datasets compare gradient norms, oracle calls, and CPU time at condition numbers 100 and 1000. The method requires exact Hessian access to the original objective, nested saddle solves, and prescribed accuracy and regularization. These structured minimax guarantees are not evidence of scalable stochastic or billion-parameter training superiority. |
| 2026-10-14 | [Memory-Efficient Subspace Optimization with Residual Correction: From Momentum SGD to Muon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19844) | Maintains momentum in a low-dimensional gradient subspace while adding a state-free orthogonal residual, yielding SMRC for momentum SGD and PMRC for Muon. PMRC polar-normalizes projected momentum, then combines its lifted direction with a scaled residual gradient; periodically refreshed bases track changing gradients. Under standard stochastic assumptions, single- and multi-stage variants achieve O(T^-1/4) stationarity rates. On C4 LLaMA-1.3B, PMRC validation loss is 2.570 versus GUM 2.656 and full Muon 2.495; Qwen3-8B IFEval instruction accuracy reaches 69.06%. Practical experiments add residual clipping and approximate SVD, and select hyperparameters using downstream evaluation benchmarks, limiting clean generalization claims. |
| 2026-10-14 | [One Size Does Not Fit All: Curvature-Aware Adaptive Perturbation for SAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20415) | Adapts SAM's perturbation radius using the ratio of consecutive gradient norms: multiply or divide by a growth factor, then clip to bounds. This reuses SAM's gradients rather than computing Hessian curvature or adding backward passes. Three-run CIFAR and ImageNet experiments report aggregate accuracy gains of 1.14 percentage points on CIFAR-100 and 0.31 points across ImageNet evaluations, with benefits across several SAM variants. The nonconvex convergence analysis additionally requires a decaying-radius envelope and decreasing learning rate; it does not directly certify the bounded experimental controller. Initial radius and growth factor still require tuning, and experiments do not establish language-model benefits. |
| 2026-10-14 | [Recursive Gradient Retraction Methods for Stochastic Nonconvex Optimization on Stiefel Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19944) | RGR constructs recursive stochastic estimators in ambient Euclidean space, maps them to the Stiefel tangent space, and retracts the update, avoiding explicit vector transport. SPIDER, stochastic recursive momentum, and EMA variants trade estimator accuracy against gradient work; adaptive steps avoid needing a supplied Lipschitz constant, with horizon-free guarantees for the online variants. Experiments evaluate PCA, low-rank completion, and orthogonal CNNs on real datasets and small vision tasks. The methods still incur tangent projection and retraction costs, while SPIDER uses periodic full-gradient refreshes. Baselines have different retraction and tuning choices, so reported gains do not isolate estimator construction in every comparison. |
| 2026-10-13 | [Composite and Stochastic Smoothed Decentralized Bregman Proximal-Gradient Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18231) [Code](https://github.com/shengze-xu/composite-stochastic-sdbpg) | This extension adds node-specific convex nonsmooth regularizers and stochastic local gradients to Bregman primal-dual smoothing with edge-based consensus. It isolates temporal subgradient motion and aggregation mismatch as defect sequences, obtaining an inverse-iteration best squared proximal-residual bound under finite accumulated defects and summable stochastic variance. Imaging, sparse recovery, and phase-retrieval experiments compare reconstruction and communication costs. Fourier imaging reaches a common target with fewer rounds than gradient-tracking baselines, but stochastic-recovery baselines reach their relaxed target earlier. Compact invariant-domain assumptions and active-structure conditions restrict guarantees; reduced communication does not imply universally lower wall-clock time or better reconstruction. |
| 2026-10-13 | [First-order Optimization Methods with Generalized Smoothness for Low-rank Matrix Factorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19059) | This work derives generalized-smoothness step rules for low-rank symmetric factorization, including adaptive Armijo gradient descent and SafeAdam. SafeAdam accepts an adaptive direction only when a descent-angle certificate holds, otherwise reverting to a normalized gradient; a projected variant handles nonnegativity. Experiments on TDT2, Reuters, and CBCL compare iteration counts, time, and factorization objectives. On CBCL, SafeAdam reaches the reported tolerance in 6764 iterations versus Dyn-NoLips's 9115, but it is slower than simpler adaptive GD on text datasets. Guarantees rely on the factorized problem's generalized smoothness and certified steps, not on unrestricted stochastic Adam convergence or large-language-model training. |
| 2026-10-13 | [How Low Can You Go: Fine-Tuning LLMs with Bayesian Optimization (LlMBO)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18504) | LlMBO combines TinyLoRA's ultra-low-dimensional adapters with trust-region Bayesian optimization, a moment-matched logit observation model for pass counts, and dimension-scaled Gaussian-process priors. Inference-only scoring can optimize greedy-decoding accuracy directly, unlike the evaluated policy-gradient objective. Experiments span ten 1.5B–8B models and 2098 runs, finding competitive gains with at most four trainable scalars and roughly 30% of GRPO's training FLOPs. This is a specialized low-dimensional fine-tuning method, not a general full-parameter optimizer. Search bounds depend on a GRPO-derived displacement budget; performance weakens as dimension grows, and greedy-objective comparisons must be distinguished from matched-temperature comparisons. |
| 2026-10-13 | [NBCLT: Parameter-Agnostic Norm-Ball Clipped Local Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17913) | Introduces federated and decentralized local training with control variates, a one-gradient predict-and-correct recurrence, and Euclidean projections onto configurable norm balls. Spectral blocks clip singular values rather than orthogonalize them; PolyGram-RCClip approximates this projection using an offline Gram-polynomial coefficient table. Under smoothness and bounded gradient variance, parameter-agnostic communication/sample bounds of O(epsilon^-2)/O(epsilon^-4) require local-computation budgets growing with the horizon, not fixed local steps. CIFAR-10 federated ResNet-18 experiments favor tuned NBCLT, but hyperparameters are selected using test accuracy. No large-language-model or decentralized empirical validation is provided, and the fixed-degree projector's accumulated residual is not certified. |
| 2026-10-13 | [Same Function, Different Trajectory: No Standard Optimizer Is Equivariant to Transformer Reparameterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18085) | Adds a function-preserving rebalancing wrapper to standard optimizers by reciprocally rescaling exact Transformer symmetry pairs: Q/K, V/O, ReLU up/down, or SwiGLU value/output. Hard SAGGS equalizes paired Frobenius norms; Soft SAGGS applies damped corrections to reduce disruption when momentum states remain unchanged. A separate canonical restart jointly transforms weights and Adam moments. In seven-seed 123.8M checkpoint-resume tests, imposed scale imbalance worsens perplexity from 20.84 to 24.62, while either wrapper restores 20.85. Gains mainly demonstrate recovery from deliberate rescaling; unperturbed improvements are small or absent. GELU MLP scaling is excluded because it is not an exact symmetry. Reference code appears in the appendix, without a verified repository. |
| 2026-10-12 | [A Feasible Direction Method with Active-Set Identification for Derivative-Free Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17101) | FDM-AS builds linear objective and constraint models inside a derivative-free trust-region method, using a feasible-direction subproblem to favor feasible trials. An active-set estimate on an expanded neighborhood reduces the constraints entering subproblems, with a lazy-model practical variant reducing evaluation work. The analysis establishes Fritz-John accumulation points, KKT points under constraint qualification, and finite identification under nondegeneracy. Small CUTEst experiments report more feasible trials and better evaluation-budget performance than matched SQP-subproblem variants. A feasible starting point is required; six benchmarks lacking a constructed feasible start were discarded. Practical lazy updates and independently charged constraint oracles limit the scope of cost comparisons. |
| 2026-10-12 | [Efficient Preconditioned Optimization Methods with Frequent Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16792) | This expanded study adds one-sided S-ASGO to Frequent-Directions adaptive preconditioning, retaining accumulated discarded mass as isotropic correction and deriving improved primal-dual and mirror-descent regret bounds. A practical EMA-and-momentum variant uses approximate singular subspaces and RMS-scaled updates. CIFAR experiments and 2400-step GPT-2 pretraining show comparable loss to fuller preconditioners with smaller sketched state; the GPT-2 study spans sequence lengths 256 and 512. Core FTSL methods already appeared at ICML 2025, so the recent contribution is the added algorithms, offline guarantees, and experiments. Sketch size trades quality against cost, and the paper-linked ASGO repository is a baseline rather than verified new-method code. |
| 2026-10-12 | [Matching Complexity for Second-Order Stationary Point with Sample Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16832) | This stochastic optimizer checks estimated curvature before spending work on gradients, taking randomly signed negative-curvature steps when needed and descent steps otherwise. Separate binary-ancestor derivative estimators retain checkpoints and reusable sample handles for gradients and complete Hessians. Truncated difference estimates distinguish predictable drift from martingale motion, yielding near-matching epsilon-to-the-minus-three plus curvature-tolerance-to-the-minus-three derivative-query complexity. The result concerns high-dimensional worst-case second-order stationarity, not a practical large-model speed comparison. Sample-wise gradient and Hessian smoothness, almost-sure derivative-noise bounds, bounded population gradients, and public parameter bounds are assumed; both endpoints of same-sample differences and full-Hessian evaluations are explicitly charged. |
| 2026-10-12 | [Matching Full-Hessian Oracle Complexity for Nonconvex-Strongly-Concave Minimax Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16771) | This minimax construction combines saddle-Moreau-envelope gradient or cubic-regularization steps with certified accelerated reduced-dual solves and warm starts amortized across outer iterations. Schur-complement formulas approximate envelope gradients and Hessians from full-Hessian access to the original objective. The resulting accuracy regimes match randomized lower bounds up to logarithmic factors and initialization costs, improving on first-order complexity where curvature regularity helps. It is an algorithmic oracle-complexity result, not a measured optimizer for large networks. Strong dual concavity, Lipschitz gradients and Hessians, prescribed accuracy, and nested certification are required; matrix inversions, linear solves, and cubic subproblem arithmetic are not represented by derivative-query counts alone. |
| 2026-10-12 | [Matching Randomized Bounds for Finite-Sum Nonconvex-Concave Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16991) | These finite-sum minimax methods regularize the dual variable and combine proximal primal updates with either a persistent gradient estimator and independent primal averaging or accelerated dual proximal solves with explicit primal certificates. Reusing anchor estimates across centers controls finite-sum initialization cost, while smoothing stability translates progress to stationarity of the original value function's Moreau envelope. The resulting query regimes match randomized lower bounds across dual-domain diameters up to stated logarithms. This is theoretical algorithm construction, not measured large-model training. Compact known dual domains, mean-squared averaged smoothness, calibrated regularization, large prescribed inner budgets, and Moreau rather than ordinary gradient stationarity constrain interpretation. |
| 2026-10-12 | [Monarch-Muon: Structure-Aware Muon for Fast and Memory-Efficient Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17429) [Code](https://github.com/brain-lab-research/monarch-muon) | Reparameterizes selected dense weights as products of block-diagonal Monarch factors and applies Muon independently to their smaller trainable blocks, reducing weights, gradients, momentum storage, and orthogonalization cost without imposing low rank. Exact-polar convergence follows a blockwise spectral trust-region interpretation. At fixed two-block structure, validation loss improves from Monarch-AdamW's 3.09 to 3.06 on the 257M reference and from 2.77 to 2.68 on the 1B MoE. Four-block 6.89B-reference systems tests reduce optimizer time from 1288 to 304 ms and peak memory from 68.9 to 52.9 GB. Quality runs are single-seed; large-scale timings change parameterization rather than establish matched-quality speedups. Released code omits MoE pretraining experiments. |
| 2026-10-12 | [Spectral-Driven Adaptive-Rank Optimizer for Large Language Model Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16533) [Code](https://github.com/RUCAIBox/Ares) | Targets reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards by estimating gradient spectral entropy, smoothing the implied rank, and truncating matrix gradients within a preset maximum-rank budget before dense AdamW updates. Randomized block power iteration avoids full SVD; vectors retain AdamW. In 300-step GRPO experiments on Qwen3-1.7B/4B/8B, seven-benchmark mean mathematical accuracy exceeds AdamW by 5.0/2.7/2.3 percentage points, with equal-budget learning-rate searches and additional RLVR objectives tested. Dense moment states remain, and no fully compute-matched speedup is established. Gradient truncation does not guarantee a strictly low-rank final adaptive update; the public rank-EMA implementation also differs from the paper's accumulator pseudocode. |
| 2026-10-12 | [StructZO: Structured Zeroth-Order Optimization for Memory-Efficient LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16451) | Constructs low-dimensional search spaces from forward-activation Gram sketches, aggregates multiple zeroth-order perturbation queries in these coordinates, applies reduced-space Muon spectral shaping, and lifts updates back to dense weights. Its estimator analysis trades reduced dimension against activation-subspace projection bias. On limited-data Llama3.1-8B tasks, average score reaches 82.9 versus LOZO 76.3 and first-order Adam 84.1; reported peak memory is 20.80 versus Adam 85.29 GiB. The recipe uses 100 queries for 400 steps rather than 20K single-query updates. Wall-clock totals are extrapolated from short timing samples; experiments focus on GLUE/SuperGLUE-style fine-tuning, without reported multi-seed uncertainty or a verified official repository. |
| 2026-10-12 | [Which Geometry on Which Layer? Measured Sign-Spectral Assignment in Mixed-Optimizer Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17285) | Uses gradient alignments and Hessian-vector products to score sign versus spectral directions, routing embedding/output groups while keeping hidden matrices on Muon. Spectral warmup, EMA smoothing, two confirmations, and one-way locking stabilize the gate. A three-seed 31M GPT-2 comparison improves matched-momentum Scion validation loss from 5.084 to 4.955. However, a separate five-seed follow-up favors static spectral assignments over G-Scion, 4.92 versus 5.45; AdamW-edge/Muon also remains stronger in a practical comparison. Approximately 4% overhead was measured in one configuration. Theory concerns ideal memoryless steps, not trajectory superiority; large-scale routing evidence and submitted implementation completeness remain limited. |
| 2026-10-10 | [Adaptive two-points zero-order accelerated methods for stochastic convex optimization problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15202) | These accelerated zeroth-order methods adapt scalar step sizes, minibatch allocation, and smoothing radii from observed function values, without supplied smoothness or variance constants. Two-point estimators, independent pilot and update batches, and explicit budget accounting support smooth and nonsmooth convex guarantees; optional extensions handle unknown solution distance and bounded evaluation corruption. The construction stores linear-dimensional optimizer state and spends two function values per paired sample, with extra adaptive sampling stages. It is an algorithmic theory contribution, not an LLM fine-tuning comparison. Geometry bounds, oracle moment assumptions, initial scales, and corruption information remain relevant; fixed smoothing or corruption can impose residual error floors. |
| 2026-10-10 | [Multilevel Monte Carlo for Zeroth-Order Stochastic Bilevel Optimization with Inexact Lower Level Solves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15047) | ML-DFO combines Gaussian-smoothed outer updates with a multilevel hierarchy of inexact lower-level solves. Coupled neighboring levels reduce correction variance, allowing many cheap coarse solves and fewer expensive fine ones; the analysis tracks solver bias, level-difference decay, and work separately. Field-of-Experts denoising and sparse-view tomography experiments report comparable reconstruction with reduced cumulative lower-level iterations. This is derivative-free bilevel parameter learning, not a general pretrained-model optimizer. Gains depend on suitable coupling and error-versus-cost decay. Practical experiments modify estimator and lower-solver schedules for efficiency, so they illustrate multilevel behavior rather than directly verify every theoretical asymptotic rate. |
| 2026-10-10 | [Parameter-Free Adaptive Extragradient Methods for Generalized-Smooth Variational Inequalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14839) | PF-AEG combines projected extragradient updates with a three-way stepsize rule controlling gradual recovery, observed local operator variation, and operator magnitude. AEG-VR adds variance reduction and randomized snapshot updates for finite-sum problems. Under generalized smoothness and quasi-sharpness, the analysis gives full-sequence convergence and last-iterate rates, including linear convergence across a broader sharpness range. Synthetic VIs, logistic regression, phase retrieval, and distributionally robust optimization illustrate favorable iteration or reported-sample progress. Parameter-free means unknown problem constants are unnecessary, not that initial stepsizes or mixing settings disappear. Projection and repeated operator evaluations remain costs, and these structured guarantees do not cover unrestricted nonconvex neural objectives. |
| 2026-10-10 | [Zeroth-Order Fine-Tuning of LLMs in Learnable Low-Rank Subspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15225) | AdaSub-ZO learns layerwise low-rank perturbation bases using forward-only finite differences, stabilizes basis learning with EMA, and orthogonalizes with QR. It maintains momentum in the small coordinate space and transfers it when bases change. Three-run OPT-1.3B experiments with rank 36 raise SST-2 accuracy from MeZO's 89.4% to 92.0%, but WiC and WSC do not improve. Basis learning consumes additional forward queries, so iteration counts alone do not measure efficiency. Full-gradient convergence assumes uniform subspace coverage that the learning procedure does not itself prove, and finite smoothing leaves an error floor. Evidence remains limited to small-task language-model fine-tuning. |
| 2026-10-09 | [Adaptive Variance Reduction without Knowing Oracle Smoothness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13799) | A known-budget method probes two independent gradients, switches from direct normalized updates to normalized momentum when two sampled gradients differ beyond a threshold, then runs a normalized-STORM phase and validates two reservoir-selected candidates. Without a known horizon, parallel variance-reduction and AdaGrad-Norm paths use doubling validation blocks. Without knowing smoothness or variance constants, expected gradient-norm rates are O(T^-1/3) under mean-square smoothness, O(T^-1/4) under ordinary smoothness, and O(T^-1/2) without noise; the anytime stochastic rates add logarithms. Assumptions include lower-bounded smooth objectives and unbiased bounded-variance gradients, with repeated-sample oracle access for variance reduction. Guarantees concern selected candidates, not final-iterate quality; no experiments or official code are provided. |
| 2026-10-09 | [Geometry-Aware Multi-Objective Optimization: Norm-Constrained Oracles with Entropic Weight Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13773) | Combines one norm-constrained oracle on blended task gradients with entropic mirror updates of simplex task weights and optional entropy regularization and weight floors. One persistent blended-momentum buffer gives stochastic O(T^-1/4) Pareto stationarity under bounded-gradient assumptions; the unregularized deterministic rate is O(T^-1/2). Three-seed MultiMNIST accuracy is 90.54% versus linear-scalarized Muon's 87.75%, but near-uniform weighting reaches 91.11%, so gains cannot be assigned to adaptive weights alone. Dense-prediction comparisons use one seed and tune on the reporting split. Practical five-step Newton-Schulz errors are not uniformly certified by the theory, and no official implementation is linked. |
| 2026-10-09 | [Optimal Algorithms for Nonsmooth Nonconvex Stochastic Optimization with Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13787) | This work extends online-to-nonconvex conversion to closed convex constraints using local online learners and randomized segment queries. Projection-based anchored gradient descent and projection-free Frank-Wolfe constructions yield Goldstein-style stationarity guarantees; two-point smoothing supplies zeroth-order counterparts. The first-order query rate matches the unconstrained nonsmooth benchmark up to logarithmic factors, while zeroth-order access introduces dimension dependence. The contribution is a theoretical constrained algorithm family rather than an experimental AdamW replacement. Stationarity notions account for boundaries and differ from ordinary gradient norms; projections or linear-minimization calls, local learner geometry, Lipschitz assumptions, and smoothing bias must be included when interpreting practicality and cost. |
| 2026-10-09 | [Partially Informed Stochastic Optimization via Zeroth-Order Residual Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13871) | Piso uses known partial-gradient information and tracks only the residual needed to recover the full gradient. Each two-point probe is centered on the current residual estimate, so sketch variance depends on tracking error rather than reconstructing the unknown component from scratch; Piso2 adds known-component averaging and estimator mixing. Experiments span pricing, strategic classification, performative RL, routing, and security games. A step uses two function-query batches and known-component samples, not free gradients. Guarantees require smoothness and independent probe conditions; an experimentally tested cycling orthonormal-frame variant falls outside the stated independence assumptions, and privileged-response-gradient baselines access additional information. |
| 2026-10-09 | [Randomized Momentum Transport with Cheap Predictors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13789) | Queries a gradient-difference or Hessian-vector momentum correction only with probability q, importance-weighting its residual around a cheap predictor to preserve the conditional mean. Smoothness, transport-bias and residual-control assumptions yield O(T^-1/3) stationarity for fixed q with normalized, sign or exact-polar LMO directions. At matched oracle budgets, five-seed CIFAR-10 HVP transport gains 1.52 accuracy points at q=1/4, but STORM loses 5.69 points. The learned diagonal predictor shows no resolved accuracy benefit. Neural experiments use Euclidean, not Muon, directions, and randomized HVP runs take 1046 versus 934 seconds despite equal oracle work. Code is promised with the final version. |
| 2026-10-08 | [Accelerated Proximal Gradient Method for Non-Strongly Convex Time-Varying Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11425) | TV-APG replaces iteration-indexed acceleration with momentum computed from current iterate displacement and uses a modified proximal step to preserve domain feasibility. For convex composite objectives on compact domains with bounded temporal variation, it improves the asymptotic tracking-gap dependence from the square-root variation rate of proximal gradient to a two-thirds power. TV-APGe additionally estimates variation through function evaluations and can perform better empirically. Tests include synthetic quartic minimization and Electricity, Traffic, and Solar autoregression, matching ten gradient queries per round. Smoothness and diameter information remain inputs, and the estimate-based guarantee requires sufficiently informative variation estimates; this is not arbitrary nonstationary neural-training theory. |
| 2026-10-08 | [Composite Online-to-Nonconvex Conversion with Optimal Oracle Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12328) | Extends online-to-nonconvex conversion to nonsmooth nonconvex objectives plus a convex regularizer, retaining the full regularizer in online losses rather than linearizing it. Composite mirror descent selects directions around fixed block anchors; stochastic interpolation queries and a final convex-model shift yield proximal Goldstein stationarity with O(delta^-1 epsilon^-3) gradient queries or O(d delta^-1 epsilon^-3) function evaluations. Ten paired trials on synthetic ReLU-regression and ramp-loss problems at dimensions 16/256/1024 show lower objectives/stationarity than Chen's minibatch/variance-reduced methods at equal query counts. Stationarity is numerically upper-bounded, frequent convex subproblems add computational overhead, and large-model/wall-clock evidence or official code is absent. |
| 2026-10-08 | [Federated Zeroth-Order Optimization with Direction Aggregation and Variance Reduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11821) | FedZOO aggregates zeroth-order directions evaluated along projected local trajectories, then makes one projected server update instead of averaging client models. FedVRZO retains this recursion but uses independent sample-direction pairs to improve variance scaling at a fixed local query budget. Five-seed CIFAR-10 black-box attack experiments use five fully participating clients; after 300 rounds, FedVRZO reaches attack loss 0.639 versus DZO-SVRG's 0.771. Theoretical query improvements concern the sampling component and smoothed projected stationarity, while local drift and heterogeneity remain. Growing sampling budgets and shrinking local steps enter convergence guarantees, and communication-matched attack results are not universal query- or wall-clock-matched training gains. |
| 2026-10-08 | [Matching the Lower Bounds: Stochastic Contracting Cubic Newton and Its Optimal Acceleration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11693) | Stochastic Contracting Cubic Newton solves regularized local models over a compact feasible set and contracts the next iterate toward the model solution. Its accelerated variant combines gradient-noise averaging, Hessian-error control, and higher-order acceleration to match the stochastic convex lower-bound exponents. The accelerated error separates gradient noise at inverse-square-root iteration order, Hessian noise at inverse-square order, and the exact term at inverse-three-and-a-half order. These guarantees concern convex objectives with Lipschitz Hessians and bounded-domain geometry, not arbitrary neural losses. Stochastic derivatives, calibrated regularization, and model-subproblem solutions are required; oracle optimality alone does not establish wall-clock superiority for large models. |
| 2026-10-08 | [Optimal Rates for Federated Variational Inequalities under different relaxations of bounded variance assumption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12641) | This communication-budgeted algorithm family combines local extragradient trajectories, worker averaging, common-query minibatches, and, for composite problems, accelerated mirror-prox sliding with local monotone solves. Its contribution is the budgeted construction and interpolation-sensitive guarantees rather than a new elementary gradient kernel. Strong-growth and root-noise models yield faster stochastic terms, while composite interpolation separates accelerated gradient, local monotone, and pooled sampling contributions. The work is an oracle-complexity study rather than an LLM training benchmark. Workers share operator and sampling assumptions, problem constants enter schedules, and exact monotone evaluations can be required. Local sequential depth and total sample count must not be conflated with communication rounds. |

Its practical sparse momentum keeps a small FP8 heavy-hitter history per column and uses in-backward updates to release dense gradients. Accuracy and throughput trade-offs remain: on OPT-13B SST-2 it reaches 94.2% at 276 tokens/s versus Adafactor's 95.5% at 350. Experiments use gradient accumulation one; pretraining is untested. |
| 2026-10-01 | [Trust the Direction, Search the Step: Zero-and-First-Order Methods for LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02190) [Code](https://github.com/nizswan/Zeroth-First-Order-Framework) | ZFO preserves a first-order optimizer's direction and uses two symmetric forward probes to fit a bounded one-dimensional Taylor or Pade model for step selection. Existing backward information supplies the directional derivative, avoiding Hessian-vector products but retaining first-order activation memory. In three-seed Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B experiments, the quadratic variant raises MATH accuracy from AdamW's 31.77% to 40.10%. The reported Qwen runtime overhead is about 5.2%, with higher peak memory. Higher-order models do not consistently help: cubic Taylor performs substantially worse on MATH. Guarantees require aligned directions and control of step-selection error, rather than ensuring universally better fine-tuning. |

A dual Bregman length-distortion condition yields O(1/k^2) convex and accelerated linear relatively strongly convex rates without requiring the distortion constant as input. D-optimal design and Poisson inverse-problem experiments examine iteration and oracle efficiency; on convex housing design it performs similarly to the best accelerated Bregman baselines. Relative strong convexity remains a required problem input, and accelerated guarantees exclude general added nonsmooth terms. Backtracking and domain-specific Bregman maps add cost, and neural-network training is not evaluated. |

Its CIFAR-10 CNN reaches 74.55% INT2 accuracy versus Adam+STE's 76.09%, and also trails Adam at INT4/INT8; the surrogate-objective KL analysis gives neighborhood rather than exact quantized-objective convergence. #### August 2026

| Date | Paper Title | Summary |
| :---: | :---: | :--- |
| 2026-08-31 | [An Inexact Augmented Lagrangian Method for (L_0, L_1)-Smooth Convex Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00261) | This method adapts inexact augmented Lagrangian optimization to linearly constrained convex objectives with gradient-dependent smoothness. Inner subproblems preserve generalized smoothness; clipped gradient descent first reaches a controlled region, then accelerated AGMsDR refines the solution. Penalty and inner-accuracy choices yield ergodic objective and feasibility guarantees, with O(1/epsilon) gradient complexity for fixed outer iteration count under stated boundedness assumptions. A synthetic constrained test compares three inner solvers: ordinary gradient descent is already fast, while two-stage acceleration improves accuracy at outer iterates. Large penalties worsen conditioning, and the paper does not demonstrate deep-network training or universal practical superiority. |
| 2026-08-31 | [Dec-BFTRL: Squre-Root Regret for Decentralized Online Upper-Linearizable Optimization under Separation Access with Application to Continuous Submodular Maximization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30271) | Dec-BFTRL combines decentralized barrier follow-the-regularized-leader, approximate gauge projection through a separation oracle, and locally certified HybridNewton solves. Agents exchange only cumulative surrogate-gradient dual vectors; curvature and feasibility work remain local. The September revision proves expected network-aggregate approximate regret of order sqrt(T) up to logarithms, using T neighbor exchanges and near-linear total separation calls per agent. Adaptive Scheduling for Norm-Constrained Optimizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05813) | Assuming curvature falls polynomially with the suboptimality gap, this paper derives an LMO learning rate η=Δ/K(Δ) that rises then decays while retaining O(1/T) deterministic convergence, and turns it into a loss-driven scheduler that selects its warmup endpoint once. | 2026-01-30 | [Mano: Restriking Manifold Optimization for LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.23000) [Code](https://github.com/hujiyo/mano-optimizer) | Mano projects momentum onto the tangent space of normalized weights and alternates row- and column-wise Oblique-manifold normalization, preserving spectral ordering without AdamW’s second moment or Muon’s Newton–Schulz whitening. Across LLaMA/Qwen models up to 1.7B it lowers final perplexity and reaches matched loss up to 1.75× faster than Muon with momentum-level state; its convergence proof removes momentum and rotation, and compute limits prevented larger-model overtraining and broad hyperparameter tests. |

On LLaMA3-8B over 50B tokens it beats both in loss and downstream accuracy while using 49.25% less optimizer-state memory than AdamW; the design assumes an empirical fixed spike ratio, and its local PSD-Hessian bound is not an optimizer convergence proof. |
| 2026-01-30 | [Spectral Gradient Descent Mitigates Anisotropy-Driven Misalignment: A Case Study in Phase Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22652) | In an anisotropic phase-retrieval model, this work reduces GD and spectral gradient descent to signal, spurious-spike, and bulk dynamics, showing polar normalization acts like sign updates in an adaptive basis: SpecGD grows components uniformly and avoids GD’s multiplicative nuisance-spike amplification. Theorems yield dimension-independent Stage I and faster alignment, supported by power-law covariance and spiked MNIST experiments; conclusions are mainly for population dynamics on a special invariant manifold, leaving online noise, general covariance, and richer nonlinear models open. |
| 2026-01-30 | [TEON: Tensorized Orthonormalization Beyond Layer-Wise Muon for Large Language Model Pre-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.23261) | TEON stacks same-type gradients from adjacent Transformer layers into a third-order tensor, matricizes and orthogonalizes it jointly, capturing cross-layer singular-vector correlations that layerwise Muon ignores. Norm comparison gives up to a √K better convergence bound when leading singular vectors align, and GPT/LLaMA runs from 60M to 1B consistently lower perplexity; the best-case theory assumes near-shared low-rank structure, while larger stacks or extreme aspect ratios degrade approximate orthogonalization and require grouping choices. |
| 2026-01-29 | [FISMO: Fisher-Structured Momentum-Orthogonalized Optimizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21750) | FISMO alternates Kronecker Fisher-factor estimates, whitens gradients, orthogonalizes momentum in that geometry, then maps updates back, retaining curvature anisotropy that Muon discards while avoiding a full Fisher matrix. A trust-region derivation gives the closed-form structured polar step and the paper claims O(T^-1/2) nonconvex convergence; 124M GPT-2 and CIFAR-10 curves beat Muon and Shampoo, but no large-model, wall-clock, or memory study establishes that repeated inverse roots and factor updates are practical at scale. |

It proves O(T^-1/2) deterministic and O(T^-1/4) stochastic stationarity, wins CIFAR-100 accuracy and speeds PCA versus nested solvers, but costs two matrix-sign operations, trains slower than Muon on CNNs, and only matches StelLA in 8B adapter tuning. |

It preserves quadratic worst-case convergence for degree one and improves Shampoo/CNN speed plus GPT-2 Muon validation loss from 5.4523 to 4.9886; guarantees do not cover the higher-degree practical variants, and neural tests are modest, including only 200M GPT tokens. |

CIFAR-10 and 124M–1.3B language-model ablations show two or three steps nearly match SVD while running faster; guarantees still assume standard smoothness and bounded variance. |
